E.2 Policy ACR
We begin by presenting Proposition 6 and its proof, which establishes the guarantee of Algorithm 2, and then proceed to the proof of Proposition 2.
Proposition 6 (Compactness of policy ACR).
Let π\pi be an arbitrary localized policy and π~\tilde{\pi} be the corresponding approximately compact localized policy output by Algorithm 2, for each agent ii, and any (𝐬,𝐚)∈𝒮×𝒜(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}, we have
|
Qiπ~​(𝐬,𝐚)=Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚).Q_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})=Q_{i}^{\pi}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}). |
|
To prove Proposition 6, we start with some technical lemmas. Using the definition of policy ACR (Algorithm 2), we obtain Lemma 3.
Lemma 3.
Fix i∈𝒩i\in\mathscr{N}. For any j∈[dis]j\in[d^{s}_{i}], si,j∈𝐬i∘s_{i,j}\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i} if and only if either of the following holds
•
(i) si,js_{i,j} has an edge to rir_{i}, or
•
(ii) there exists i′∈𝒩i^{\prime}\in\mathscr{N} such that there exists a path from si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) to ri′​(t+l)r_{i^{\prime}}(t+l)
|
si,j(t)→si⁡(t+1),j⁡(t+1)(t+1)→si⁡(t+2),j⁡(t+2)(t+2)⋯→si⁡(t+l),j⁡(t+l)(t+l)→ri′(t+l),s_{i,j}(t)\rightarrow s_{i(t+1),j(t+1)}(t+1)\rightarrow s_{i(t+2),j(t+2)}(t+2)\cdots\rightarrow s_{i(t+l),j(t+l)}(t+l)\rightarrow r_{i^{\prime}}(t+l), |
|
where i⁡(t+l)=i′i(t+l)=i^{\prime} and i⁡(t+m)∈𝒩i⁡(t+m−1)i(t+m)\in\mathcal{N}_{i(t+m-1)} for any m∈[l]m\in[l] and for some l≥1l\geq 1. Moreover, sj⁡(t+m),i⁡(t+m)​(t+m)∈𝐬i⁡(t+m)∘s_{j(t+m),i(t+m)}(t+m)\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i(t+m)} for all m∈[l]m\in[l].
Proof of Lemma 3.
We prove this by induction on the iterations of the while loop in Algorithm 2.
Base case: After initialization (lines 2-8 of Algorithm 2), 𝐬i∘\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i} contains exactly those state components si,js_{i,j} that have a direct edge to rir_{i} (i.e., where 𝐜i,js→r=1\mathbf{c}^{s\to r}_{i,j}=1). This corresponds precisely to condition (i).
Inductive hypothesis: Suppose that after kk iterations of the while loop, for any agent ii and component jj, si,j∈𝐬i∘s_{i,j}\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i} if and only if either:
•
si,js_{i,j} has a direct edge to rir_{i}, or
•
there exists a path from si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) to some ri′​(t+m)r_{i^{\prime}}(t+m) where m≤km\leq k, such that all intermediate state components are in their respective 𝐬∘\mathbf{s}^{\circ} sets, and each consecutive agent in the path is a neighbor of the previous agent.
Inductive step: Consider the (k+1)(k+1)-th iteration. In this iteration, according to Algorithm 2 (lines 10-17), we add si,js_{i,j} to 𝐬i∘\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i} if there is an edge from si,js_{i,j} to some component of 𝐬𝒩i∘\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}, where 𝐬∘𝒩i=∪i′∈Ni𝐬∘i′\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}=\cup_{i^{\prime}\in N_{i}}\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i^{\prime}} contains all minimal components of ii’s neighbors from the previous iterations.
By the inductive hypothesis, for any i′∈𝒩ii^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{i} and any component j′j^{\prime}, si′,j′∈𝐬i′∘s_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i^{\prime}} if and only if either:
•
si′,j′s_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}} has a direct edge to ri′r_{i^{\prime}}, or
•
there exists a path from si′,j′​(t)s_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}(t) to some ri′′​(t+m)r_{i^{\prime\prime}}(t+m) where m≤km\leq k, with all intermediate components in their respective 𝐬∘\mathbf{s}^{\circ} sets.
Forward direction: Suppose si,js_{i,j} is added to 𝐬i∘\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i} in the (k+1)(k+1)-th iteration. Then there must be an edge from si,js_{i,j} to some component si′,j′∈𝐬i′∘s_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i^{\prime}} for some i′∈𝒩ii^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}. By the inductive hypothesis, either:
Case 1: If si′,j′s_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}} has a direct edge to ri′r_{i^{\prime}}, then we have a path:
|
si,j​(t)→si′,j′​(t+1)→ri′​(t+1),s_{i,j}(t)\to s_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}(t+1)\to r_{i^{\prime}}(t+1), |
|
which is a path of length 2 (l=1l=1) from si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) to ri′​(t+1)r_{i^{\prime}}(t+1), satisfying condition (ii).
Case 2: If there is a path from si′,j′​(t+1)s_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}(t+1) to ri′′​(t+1+m)r_{i^{\prime\prime}}(t+1+m) for some m≤km\leq k, then we have a longer path:
|
si,j​(t)→si′,j′​(t+1)→⋯→ri′′​(t+1+m),s_{i,j}(t)\to s_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}(t+1)\to\cdots\to r_{i^{\prime\prime}}(t+1+m), |
|
which is a path of length m+2m+2 (l=m+1≤k+1l=m+1\leq k+1) from si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) to ri′′​(t+1+m)r_{i^{\prime\prime}}(t+1+m), satisfying condition (ii).
In both cases, i′∈𝒩ii^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{i} (the neighborhood constraint is satisfied), and all intermediate state components are in their respective 𝐬∘\mathbf{s}^{\circ} sets by the inductive hypothesis.
Backward direction: Conversely, suppose there exists a path from si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) to ri′​(t+l)r_{i^{\prime}}(t+l) for some l≤k+1l\leq k+1, where all intermediate components are in their respective 𝐬∘\mathbf{s}^{\circ} sets and each agent is a neighbor of the previous agent.
If l=0l=0, then si,js_{i,j} has a direct edge to rir_{i} (condition (i)), so si,j∈𝐬i∘s_{i,j}\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i} after initialization.
If l≥1l\geq 1, consider the path:
|
si,j​(t)→si⁡(t+1),j⁡(t+1)​(t+1)→⋯→ri′​(t+l).s_{i,j}(t)\to s_{i(t+1),j(t+1)}(t+1)\to\cdots\to r_{i^{\prime}}(t+l). |
|
The first transition in this path is from si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) to si⁡(t+1),j⁡(t+1)​(t+1)s_{i(t+1),j(t+1)}(t+1), where i⁡(t+1)∈Nii(t+1)\in N_{i} (by the neighborhood constraint). By the path condition, si⁡(t+1),j⁡(t+1)​(t+1)∈𝐬i⁡(t+1)∘s_{i(t+1),j(t+1)}(t+1)\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i(t+1)}.
Furthermore, there exists a path from si⁡(t+1),j⁡(t+1)​(t+1)s_{i(t+1),j(t+1)}(t+1) to ri′​(t+l)r_{i^{\prime}}(t+l) of length l−1≤kl-1\leq k. By the inductive hypothesis, si⁡(t+1),j⁡(t+1)∈𝐬i⁡(t+1)∘s_{i(t+1),j(t+1)}\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i(t+1)}.
Since i⁡(t+1)∈𝒩ii(t+1)\in\mathcal{N}_{i}, we have si⁡(t+1),j⁡(t+1)∈𝐬𝒩i∘s_{i(t+1),j(t+1)}\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}. Therefore, there is an edge from si,js_{i,j} to a component in 𝐬𝒩i∘\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}, which means si,js_{i,j} will be added to 𝐬i∘\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i} in the (k+1)(k+1)-th iteration (or earlier).
By induction, si,j∈𝐬i∘s_{i,j}\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i} if and only if either condition (i) or condition (ii) holds, completing the proof.
∎
Lemma 4.
Fix i∈𝒩i\in\mathscr{N}. For any j∈[dis]j\in[d^{s}_{i}], si,j∉𝐬i∘s_{i,j}\not\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}, if and only if each of the following holds
•
(i) si,js_{i,j} does not have an edge to rir_{i}
•
(ii) for any i′∈𝒩i^{\prime}\in\mathscr{N}, there exists no path from si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) to ri′​(t+l)r_{i^{\prime}}(t+l) for some ll
Proof of Lemma 4.
This lemma is the logical contrapositive of Lemma 3. Let’s formalize this relationship.
Lemma 3 states that si,j∈𝐬i∘s_{i,j}\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i} if and only if either:
•
si,js_{i,j} has an edge to rir_{i}, or
•
There exists i′∈𝒩i^{\prime}\in\mathscr{N} and there exists a path from si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) to ri′​(t+l)r_{i^{\prime}}(t+l) satisfying certain constraints.
The logical form of Lemma 3 can be written as: si,j∈𝐬i∘⇔(A)∨(B)s_{i,j}\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}\iff(A)\lor(B).
The contrapositive of this statement is: si,j∉𝐬i∘⇔¬((A)∨(B))s_{i,j}\not\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}\iff\neg((A)\lor(B)).
Using De Morgan’s laws, ¬((A)∨(B))⇔¬(A)∧¬(B)\neg((A)\lor(B))\iff\neg(A)\land\neg(B), which gives us:
si,j∉𝐬i∘⇔¬(A)∧¬(B)s_{i,j}\not\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}\iff\neg(A)\land\neg(B), where:
•
¬(A)\neg(A): si,js_{i,j} does not have an edge to rir_{i}
•
¬(B)\neg(B): For all i′∈𝒩i^{\prime}\in\mathscr{N} and for all l≥1l\geq 1, there exists no path from si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) to ri′​(t+l)r_{i^{\prime}}(t+l) that satisfies the constraints on intermediate nodes.
These are precisely the conditions (i) and (ii) in Lemma 4. Therefore, by the logical equivalence of a statement and its contrapositive, Lemma 4 is proved.
∎
Proof of Proposition 6.
We will prove that the QQ-functions are identical by showing that the distribution of rewards collected under both policies is identical. This follows if we can establish that the relevant state-action trajectories have the same distribution under both policies.
Let us define:
•
ρtπ(𝐬(t),𝐚(t)|𝐬(0)=𝐬,𝐚(0)=𝐚)\rho^{\pi}_{t}(\mathbf{s}(t),\mathbf{a}(t)|\mathbf{s}(0)=\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}): The probability of being in state-action pair (𝐬⁡(t),𝐚⁡(t))(\mathbf{s}(t),\mathbf{a}(t)) at time tt, starting from (𝐬⁡(0)=𝐬,𝐚⁡(0)=𝐚)(\mathbf{s}(0)=\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}) and following policy π\pi.
•
ri​(𝐬i,𝐚i)r_{i}(\mathbf{s}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i}): The reward for agent ii given its state-action pair.
The QQ-function for agent ii under policy π\pi can be expressed as:
|
Qiπ(𝐬,𝐚)=𝔼π[∑t=0∞γtri(𝐬i(t),𝐚i(t))∣𝐬(0)=𝐬,𝐚(0)=𝐚]Q^{\pi}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}r_{i}(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t))\mid\mathbf{s}(0)=\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}\right] |
|
|
=∑t=0∞γt∑𝐬⁡(t),𝐚⁡(t)ρtπ(𝐬(t),𝐚t|𝐬(0)=𝐬,𝐚(0)=𝐚)⋅ri(𝐬i(t),𝐚i(t)).=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\sum_{\mathbf{s}(t),\mathbf{a}(t)}\rho^{\pi}_{t}(\mathbf{s}(t),\mathbf{a}_{t}|\mathbf{s}(0)=\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a})\cdot r_{i}(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t)). |
|
Similarly, we can define Qiπ~​(𝐬,𝐚)Q^{\tilde{\pi}}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}) for the approximately compact policy π~\tilde{\pi}.
To prove Qiπ~​(𝐬,𝐚)=Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚)Q^{\tilde{\pi}}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})=Q^{\pi}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}), it suffices to show that for all t≥0t\geq 0, the distribution of rewards ri​(𝐬i​(t),𝐚i​(t))r_{i}(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t)) is identical under both policies.
From Lemma 3, we know that the reward ri​(𝐬i,𝐚i)r_{i}(\mathbf{s}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i}) depends only on the components in 𝐬i∘\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i} and aia_{i}. This is because any state component not in 𝐬i∘\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i} has no path to rir_{i} (directly or indirectly), meaning it cannot influence the reward function. Therefore, we need to prove that the joint distribution of (𝐬i∘​(t),𝐚i​(t))(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t)) is the same under both π\pi and π~\tilde{\pi}.
We prove this by induction on tt.
Base case (t=0t=0): The initial state 𝐬⁡(0)=𝐬\mathbf{s}(0)=\mathbf{s} is given, so 𝐬i∘​(0)\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}(0) is the same for both policies. For the initial action, we have:
Under π\pi: 𝐚i(0)∼πi(⋅|𝐬𝒩i(0))\mathbf{a}_{i}(0)\sim\pi_{i}(\cdot|\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(0))
Under π~\tilde{\pi}: 𝐚i(0)∼π~i(⋅|𝐬𝒩i∘(0))\mathbf{a}_{i}(0)\sim\tilde{\pi}_{i}(\cdot|\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(0))
By the definition of the approximately compact localized policy:
|
π~i(⋅|𝐬𝒩i∘)=πi(⋅|𝐬𝒩i∘,𝐬𝒩i/𝐬𝒩i∘)\tilde{\pi}_{i}(\cdot|\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}})=\pi_{i}(\cdot|\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}/\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}) |
|
for any fixed 𝐬𝒩i∘\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}} and for any configuration of the non-minimal components 𝐬𝒩i/𝐬𝒩i∘\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}/\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}. Therefore, 𝐚i​(0)\mathbf{a}_{i}(0) has the same distribution under both policies when conditioned on the same initial state.
Inductive hypothesis: Assume that for some t≥0t\geq 0, the joint distribution of (𝐬i∘​(t),𝐚i​(t))(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t)) is the same under both π\pi and π~\tilde{\pi} for all agents ii.
Inductive step: We need to show that the joint distribution of (𝐬i∘​(t+1),𝐚i​(t+1))(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}(t+1),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t+1)) is also the same under both policies.
For 𝐬i∘​(t+1)\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}(t+1), we analyze how it depends on the previous state and action. By the Markov assumption, 𝐬⁡(t+1)\mathbf{s}(t+1) depends only on 𝐬⁡(t)\mathbf{s}(t) and 𝐚⁡(t)\mathbf{a}(t). The key insight is to show that 𝐬i∘​(t+1)\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}(t+1) depends only on 𝐬𝒩i∘​(t)\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t) and 𝐚𝒩i​(t)\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t), not on any component outside 𝐬𝒩i∘​(t)\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t).
Suppose, for contradiction, that there exists a component sk,j​(t)∉𝐬𝒩i∘​(t)s_{k,j}(t)\not\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t) that influences some component si,j′​(t+1)∈𝐬i∘​(t+1)s_{i,j^{\prime}}(t+1)\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}(t+1). This means there is a causal link from sk,j​(t)s_{k,j}(t) to si,j′​(t+1)s_{i,j^{\prime}}(t+1).
By Lemma 3, since si,j′​(t+1)∈𝐬i∘​(t+1)s_{i,j^{\prime}}(t+1)\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}(t+1), either:
•
si,j′​(t+1)s_{i,j^{\prime}}(t+1) has a direct edge to rir_{i}, or
•
There exists a path from si,j′​(t+1)s_{i,j^{\prime}}(t+1) to some reward ri′​(t+1+l)r_{i^{\prime}}(t+1+l) for some l≥0l\geq 0.
Either way, this creates a path from sk,j​(t)s_{k,j}(t) to a reward (either directly to rir_{i} through si,j′​(t+1)s_{i,j^{\prime}}(t+1), or to ri′​(t+1+l)r_{i^{\prime}}(t+1+l) via the path from si,j′​(t+1)s_{i,j^{\prime}}(t+1)).
This path satisfies the constraints in Lemma 3 condition (ii), which would imply sk,j​(t)∈𝐬k∘​(t)s_{k,j}(t)\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{k}(t). Since k∈𝒩ik\in\mathcal{N}_{i} (there is a causal link from agent kk to agent ii), we would have sk,j​(t)∈𝐬𝒩i∘​(t)s_{k,j}(t)\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t), contradicting our assumption.
Therefore, 𝐬i∘​(t+1)\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}(t+1) depends only on 𝐬𝒩i∘​(t)\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t) and 𝐚𝒩i​(t)\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t). By the inductive hypothesis, the joint distribution of (𝐬𝒩i∘​(t),𝐚𝒩i​(t))(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t),\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t)) is the same under both policies, so the distribution of 𝐬i∘​(t+1)\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}(t+1) is also the same.
For 𝐚i​(t+1)\mathbf{a}_{i}(t+1), we have:
Under π\pi: 𝐚i(t+1)∼πi(⋅|𝐬𝒩i(t+1))\mathbf{a}_{i}(t+1)\sim\pi_{i}(\cdot|\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t+1))
Under π~\tilde{\pi}: 𝐚i(t+1)∼π~i(⋅|𝐬𝒩i∘(t+1))\mathbf{a}_{i}(t+1)\sim\tilde{\pi}_{i}(\cdot|\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t+1))
By the definition of approximately compact policy and the fact that the distribution of 𝐬𝒩i∘​(t+1)\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t+1) is the same under both policies, 𝐚i​(t+1)\mathbf{a}_{i}(t+1) also has the same distribution under both policies.
By induction, for all t≥0t\geq 0, the joint distribution of (𝐬i∘​(t),𝐚i​(t))(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t)) is the same under both π\pi and π~\tilde{\pi} for all agents ii.
Since the reward ri​(𝐬i​(t),𝐚i​(t))r_{i}(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t)) depends only on 𝐬i∘​(t)\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}(t) and 𝐚i​(t)\mathbf{a}_{i}(t), and these have the same distribution under both policies, the expected discounted sum of rewards is also the same.
Therefore, Qiπ~​(𝐬,𝐚)=Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚)Q^{\tilde{\pi}}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})=Q^{\pi}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}) for all agents ii and all state-action pairs (𝐬,𝐚)(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}).
∎
Corollary 1.
Let π\pi be an arbitrary localized policy and π~\tilde{\pi} be the corresponding approximately compact localized policy output by Algorithm 2, for each agent ii, and any (𝐬,𝐚)∈𝒮×𝒜(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}, the approximation error between Q~iπ~​(𝐬𝒩iκ∘,𝐚𝒩iκ)\tilde{Q}_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}}\right) and Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚)Q_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\right) can be bounded as
|
sup(𝐬,𝐚)∈𝒮×𝒜|Q~iπ~​(𝐬𝒩iκ∘,𝐚𝒩iκ)−Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚)|≤r¯1−γ​γκ+1.\displaystyle\sup_{(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left\lvert\tilde{Q}_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}}\right)-Q_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\right)\right\rvert\leq\frac{\bar{r}}{1-\gamma}\gamma^{\kappa+1}. |
|
Proof.
Using Proposition 6, we have
|
|Q~iπ~​(𝐬𝒩iκ∘,𝐚𝒩iκ)−Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚)|\displaystyle\left\lvert\tilde{Q}_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}}\right)-Q_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\right)\right\rvert |
≤|Q~iπ~​(𝐬𝒩iκ∘,𝐚𝒩iκ)−Qiπ~​(𝐬,𝐚)|+|Qiπ~​(𝐬,𝐚)−Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚)|\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\tilde{Q}_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}}\right)-Q_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\right)\right\rvert+\left\lvert Q_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\right)-Q_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\right)\right\rvert |
|
|
|
≤r¯1−γ​γκ+1.\displaystyle\leq\frac{\bar{r}}{1-\gamma}\gamma^{\kappa+1}. |
|
∎
Proposition 7 (Approximation error of policy ACR).
For any policy π\pi, let π~\tilde{\pi} be the corresponding approximately compact policy constructed using Algorithm 3, for any ii, we have
|
sup(𝐬,𝐚)∈𝒮×𝒜|Qiπ~​(𝐬,𝐚)−Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚)|≤r¯1−γ​γκ+1.\displaystyle\sup_{(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left\lvert Q_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\right)-Q_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\right)\right\rvert\leq\frac{\bar{r}}{1-\gamma}\gamma^{\kappa+1}. |
|
Proof of Proposition 7.
Let us first establish precise definitions:
•
Let 𝐬i∘,∞\mathbf{s}^{\circ,\infty}_{i} denote the minimal state representation for agent ii obtained from Algorithm 2 (with indefinite recursion until convergence).
•
Let 𝐬i∘,κ\mathbf{s}^{\circ,\kappa}_{i} denote the minimal state representation for agent ii obtained from Algorithm 3 (with exactly κ\kappa steps).
•
Let π\pi be the original localized (1-hop) policy.
•
Let π~∞\tilde{\pi}^{\infty} be the approximately compact policy derived from Algorithm 2.
•
Let π~κ\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa} be the approximately compact policy derived from Algorithm 3 with κ\kappa steps.
Relationship between minimal representations: By construction of the algorithms, we have 𝐬i∘,κ⊆𝐬i∘,∞\mathbf{s}^{\circ,\kappa}_{i}\subseteq\mathbf{s}^{\circ,\infty}_{i} for any finite κ\kappa. This is because Algorithm 3 performs exactly κ\kappa iterations, while Algorithm 2 continues until convergence, which may require more than κ\kappa iterations.
Lemma 5.
For any agent ii, 𝐬i∘,κ\mathbf{s}^{\circ,\kappa}_{i} captures all state components that can influence rewards within κ\kappa time steps.
Proof.
By the structure of Algorithm 3, in step 1, 𝐬i∘​(1)\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}(1) includes all state components that directly affect the reward rir_{i}. In each subsequent step ll (for l=1,2,…,κ−1l=1,2,...,\kappa-1), any state component that can affect a component in 𝐬i∘​(l)\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}(l) within one time step is added to 𝐬i∘​(l+1)\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}(l+1). By induction, after κ\kappa steps, 𝐬i∘​(κ)\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{i}(\kappa) contains all state components that can affect the reward within κ\kappa time steps.
∎
Policy equivalence and divergence: From Proposition 6, we know that for the indefinite recursion case:
|
Qiπ~∞​(𝐬,𝐚)=Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚).Q^{\tilde{\pi}^{\infty}}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})=Q^{\pi}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}). |
|
Therefore, to prove Proposition 7, we need to show:
|
sup(𝐬,𝐚)∈S×A|Qiπ~κ​(𝐬,𝐚)−Qiπ~∞​(𝐬,𝐚)|≤r¯1−γ​γκ+1.\sup_{(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})\in S\times A}|Q^{\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa}}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})-Q^{\tilde{\pi}^{\infty}}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})|\leq\frac{\bar{r}}{1-\gamma}\gamma^{\kappa+1}. |
|
Trajectory analysis: For any initial state-action pair (𝐬,𝐚)(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}), we’ll analyze the difference in trajectories under policies π~κ\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa} and π~∞\tilde{\pi}^{\infty}.
Lemma 6.
For any initial state 𝐬⁡(0)=𝐬\mathbf{s}(0)=\mathbf{s} and action 𝐚⁡(0)=𝐚\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}, the distribution of states and actions under policies π~κ\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa} and π~∞\tilde{\pi}^{\infty} are identical for the first κ\kappa time steps, i.e., for t=0,1,…,κt=0,1,...,\kappa.
Proof.
We prove this by induction.
Base case (t=0t=0): By definition, 𝐬⁡(0)=𝐬\mathbf{s}(0)=\mathbf{s} and 𝐚⁡(0)=𝐚\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a} for both policies.
Inductive step: Assume the distributions are identical for time steps 0,1,…,t0,1,...,t where t<κt<\kappa. At time tt, given identical state distributions, both policies make decisions based on the minimal state representations:
•
π~κ\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa} uses 𝐬𝒩i∘,κ​(t)\mathbf{s}^{\circ,\kappa}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t)
•
π~∞\tilde{\pi}^{\infty} uses 𝐬𝒩i∘,∞​(t)\mathbf{s}^{\circ,\infty}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t)
For any component si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) that affects rewards within κ−t\kappa-t time steps, by Lemma 5, si,j​(t)∈𝐬i∘,κ​(t)s_{i,j}(t)\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ,\kappa}_{i}(t). Since t<κt<\kappa, both policies have identical information about all state components that can affect rewards within the remaining time steps. Therefore, the action distributions at time tt are identical, which leads to identical state distributions at time t+1t+1.
∎
QQ-function decomposition: For any initial state-action pair (𝐬,𝐚)(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}), we can decompose the QQ-functions as follows:
|
Qiπ~κ​(𝐬,𝐚)\displaystyle Q^{\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa}}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}) |
=𝔼π~κ[∑t=0κγtri(𝐬i(t),𝐚i(t))∣𝐬(0)=𝐬,𝐚(0)=𝐚]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa}}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\kappa}\gamma^{t}r_{i}(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t))\mid\mathbf{s}(0)=\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}\right] |
|
|
|
+𝔼π~κ[∑t=κ+1∞γtri(𝐬i(t),𝐚i(t))∣𝐬(0)=𝐬,𝐚(0)=𝐚]\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa}}\left[\sum_{t=\kappa+1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}r_{i}(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t))\mid\mathbf{s}(0)=\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}\right] |
|
|
Qiπ~∞​(𝐬,𝐚)\displaystyle Q^{\tilde{\pi}^{\infty}}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}) |
=𝔼π~∞[∑t=0κγtri(𝐬i(t),𝐚i(t))∣𝐬(0)=𝐬,𝐚(0)=𝐚]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\infty}}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\kappa}\gamma^{t}r_{i}(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t))\mid\mathbf{s}(0)=\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}\right] |
|
|
|
+𝔼π~∞[∑t=κ+1∞γtri(𝐬i(t),𝐚i(t))∣𝐬(0)=𝐬,𝐚(0)=𝐚].\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\infty}}\left[\sum_{t=\kappa+1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}r_{i}(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t))\mid\mathbf{s}(0)=\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}\right]. |
|
By Lemma 2, the first terms are identical. Therefore:
|
|Qiπ~κ​(𝐬,𝐚)−Qiπ~∞​(𝐬,𝐚)|\displaystyle|Q^{\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa}}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})-Q^{\tilde{\pi}^{\infty}}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})| |
=|𝔼π~κ[∑t=κ+1∞γtri(𝐬i(t),𝐚i(t))∣𝐬(0)=𝐬,𝐚(0)=𝐚]\displaystyle=\left|\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa}}\left[\sum_{t=\kappa+1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}r_{i}(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t))\mid\mathbf{s}(0)=\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}\right]\right. |
|
|
|
−𝔼π~∞[∑t=κ+1∞γtri(𝐬i(t),𝐚i(t))∣𝐬(0)=𝐬,𝐚(0)=𝐚]|.\displaystyle-\left.\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\infty}}\left[\sum_{t=\kappa+1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}r_{i}(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t))\mid\mathbf{s}(0)=\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}\right]\right|. |
|
Error bound:
Let ρtπ~κ\rho^{\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa}}_{t} and ρtπ~∞\rho^{\tilde{\pi}^{\infty}}_{t} be the distributions of (𝐬i​(t),𝐚i​(t))(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t)) under policies π~κ\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa} and π~∞\tilde{\pi}^{\infty} respectively, conditioned on (𝐬⁡(0),𝐚⁡(0))=(𝐬,𝐚)(\mathbf{s}(0),\mathbf{a}(0))=(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}).
From Lemma 6, we know that ρtπ~κ=ρtπ~∞\rho^{\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa}}_{t}=\rho^{\tilde{\pi}^{\infty}}_{t} for t≤κt\leq\kappa.
For t>κt>\kappa, we can bound the total variation distance between these distributions:
|
TV​(ρtπ~κ,ρtπ~∞)≤1.\text{TV}(\rho^{\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa}}_{t},\rho^{\tilde{\pi}^{\infty}}_{t})\leq 1. |
|
Therefore:
|
|Qiπ~κ​(𝐬,𝐚)−Qiπ~∞​(𝐬,𝐚)|\displaystyle|Q^{\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa}}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})-Q^{\tilde{\pi}^{\infty}}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})| |
=|∑t=κ+1∞γt​(𝔼(𝐬i,𝐚i)∼ρtπ~κ​[ri​(𝐬i,𝐚i)]−𝔼(si,ai)∼ρtπ~∞​[ri​(𝐬i,𝐚i)])|\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{t=\kappa+1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\left(\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{s}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i})\sim\rho^{\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa}}_{t}}[r_{i}(\mathbf{s}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i})]-\mathbb{E}_{(s_{i},a_{i})\sim\rho^{\tilde{\pi}^{\infty}}_{t}}[r_{i}(\mathbf{s}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i})]\right)\right| |
|
|
|
≤∑t=κ+1∞γt​r¯⋅TV​(ρtπ~κ,ρtπ~∞)\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=\kappa+1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\bar{r}\cdot\text{TV}(\rho^{\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa}}_{t},\rho^{\tilde{\pi}^{\infty}}_{t}) |
|
|
|
≤∑t=κ+1∞γt​r¯=r¯​γκ+1​11−γ.\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=\kappa+1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\bar{r}=\bar{r}\gamma^{\kappa+1}\frac{1}{1-\gamma}. |
|
This gives us the desired bound:
|
sup(𝐬,𝐚)∈S×A|Qiπ~κ​(𝐬,𝐚)−Qiπ~∞​(𝐬,𝐚)|≤r¯1−γ​γκ+1.\sup_{(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})\in S\times A}|Q^{\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa}}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})-Q^{\tilde{\pi}^{\infty}}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})|\leq\frac{\bar{r}}{1-\gamma}\gamma^{\kappa+1}. |
|
Since Qiπ~∞​(𝐬,𝐚)=Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚)Q^{\tilde{\pi}^{\infty}}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})=Q^{\pi}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}) from Proposition 6, we have:
|
sup(𝐬,𝐚)∈S×A|Qiπ~κ​(𝐬,𝐚)−Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚)|≤r¯1−γ​γκ+1.\sup_{(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})\in S\times A}|Q^{\tilde{\pi}^{\kappa}}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})-Q^{\pi}_{i}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})|\leq\frac{\bar{r}}{1-\gamma}\gamma^{\kappa+1}. |
|
This completes the proof of Proposition 7.
∎
Proof of Proposition 2.
Using Proposition 7, we have
|
|Q~iπ~​(𝐬𝒩iκ∘,𝐚𝒩iκ)−Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚)|\displaystyle\left\lvert\tilde{Q}_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}}\right)-Q_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\right)\right\rvert |
≤|Q~iπ~​(𝐬𝒩iκ∘,𝐚𝒩iκ)−Qiπ~​(𝐬,𝐚)|+|Qiπ~​(𝐬,𝐚)−Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚)|\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\tilde{Q}_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}}\right)-Q_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\right)\right\rvert+\left\lvert Q_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\right)-Q_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\right)\right\rvert |
|
|
|
≤3​r¯1−γ​γκ+1.\displaystyle\leq\frac{3\bar{r}}{1-\gamma}\gamma^{\kappa+1}. |
|
∎
E.3 Cross domain
Denote by 𝝎𝒩iκ:=(𝝎i)i∈𝒩iκ\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}}:=(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}} and 𝝎𝒩−iκ:=𝝎/𝝎𝒩iκ\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{-i}}:=\boldsymbol{\omega}/\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}}.
Lemma 7 (Exponential decay property of domain-specific value functions).
For any localized policy π\pi, for any i∈𝒩,𝐬𝒩iκ∈𝒮𝒩iκ,𝐬𝒩−iκ,𝐬𝒩−iκ′∈𝒮𝒩−iκ,𝐚𝒩iκ∈𝒜𝒩iκ,𝐚𝒩−iκ,𝐚𝒩−iκ′∈𝒜𝒩−iκ,𝝎𝒩iκ∈Ω𝒩iκ,𝝎𝒩−iκ,𝝎′𝒩−iκ∈Ω𝒩−iκi\in\mathscr{N},\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{-i}^{\kappa}},\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{-i}^{\kappa}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{N}_{-i}^{\kappa}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{-i}^{\kappa}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{-i}^{\kappa}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}_{-i}^{\kappa}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}}\in\Omega_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{-i}},\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{-i}}\in\Omega_{\mathcal{N}_{-i}^{\kappa}}, the following holds
|
|Qiπ​(𝐬𝒩iκ,𝐬𝒩−iκ,𝐚𝒩iκ,𝐚𝒩−iκ,𝝎𝒩iκ,𝝎𝒩−iκ)−Qiπ​(𝐬𝒩iκ,𝐬𝒩−iκ′,𝐚𝒩iκ,𝐚𝒩−iκ′,𝝎𝒩iκ,𝝎𝒩−iκ′)|≤r¯1−γ​γκ.\left|Q_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{-i}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{-i}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{-i}}\right)-Q_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{-i}^{\kappa}}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{-i}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{-i}}\right)\right|\leq\frac{\bar{r}}{1-\gamma}\gamma^{\kappa}. |
|
Proof.
The proof follows from that of Lemma 1
by augmenting the state space of each agent by 𝒮i×Ωi∋(𝐬i,𝝎i)\mathcal{S}_{i}\times\Omega_{i}\ni(\mathbf{s}_{i},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}), where the augmented state variable 𝝎i\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i} degenerates to a deterministic state (𝝎i​(t)=𝝎i,∀t\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(t)=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i},\forall t).
∎
E.3.1 Value functions
Given the generative model (Equation 6), we define 𝝎\boldsymbol{\omega}-ACR for QQ-functions as follows.
Definition 5 (Value 𝝎\boldsymbol{\omega}-ACR).
Given the graphical representation of the networked system model 𝒢\mathcal{G} that is encoded in the binary masks c⋅→⋅c^{\cdot\rightarrow\cdot}, the value ACR 𝐬𝒩iκ∘\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}} (Definition 1), for each agent ii and its κ\kappa-hop neighborhood 𝒩iκ\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}, we recursively define domain-specific ACR 𝝎𝒩iκ∘\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}}: the latent change factors 𝝎𝒩iκ\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}} that either
•
𝝎i,j\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i,j} has a direct link to agent ii’s reward rir_{i}, i.e., 𝝎i,j=𝝎ir\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i,j}=\boldsymbol{\omega}^{r}_{i} and 𝐜i,j𝝎→ri=1\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}\rightarrow r_{i}}_{i,j}=1, or
•
𝝎i′,j\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i^{\prime},j} has an edge to a state component si′,l∈𝐬𝒩iκ∘s_{i^{\prime},l}\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}}, i.e., 𝐜i′,j,l𝝎→s=1\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}\rightarrow s}_{i^{\prime},j,l}=1 and si′,l∈𝐬𝒩iκ∘s_{i^{\prime},l}\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}}.
Definition 5 also defines agent-wise ACR: ∪i′∈𝒩iκ𝝎∘i′=𝝎∘𝒩iκ\cup_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}}\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\circ}_{i^{\prime}}=\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}}.
Definition 6 (Approximately compact 𝝎\boldsymbol{\omega}-conditioned QQ-function).
Fix i∈𝒩i\in\mathscr{N} and an arbitrary s¯𝒩iκ\bar{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}} and 𝝎¯𝒩iκ\bar{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}}. Define the approximately compact 𝝎\boldsymbol{\omega}-conditioned QQ-function as
|
Q~iπ​(𝐬𝒩iκ∘,𝐚𝒩iκ,𝝎𝒩iκ∘):=Q^iπ​(𝐬𝒩iκ∘,𝐬¯𝒩iκ/𝐬𝒩iκ∘,𝐚𝒩iκ,𝝎𝒩iκ∘,𝝎¯𝒩iκ/𝝎𝒩iκ∘).\tilde{Q}_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}}^{\circ}\right):=\hat{Q}_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}},\bar{\mathbf{s}}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}}/\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}}^{\circ},\bar{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}}/\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}}^{\circ}\right). |
|
Proposition 8 (Approximation error of value ACR).
For each agent ii, the approximation error between Q~iπ​(𝐬𝒩iκ∘,𝐚𝒩iκ,𝝎𝒩iκ∘)\tilde{Q}_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}}^{\circ}\right) and Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚,𝝎)Q_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{\omega}\right) can be bounded as
|
sup(𝐬,𝐚)∈𝒮×𝒜|Q~iπ​(𝐬𝒩iκ∘,𝐚𝒩iκ,𝝎𝒩iκ∘)−Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚,𝝎)|≤2​r¯1−γ​γκ+1.\displaystyle\sup_{(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left\lvert\tilde{Q}_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}}^{\circ}\right)-Q_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)\right\rvert\leq\frac{2\bar{r}}{1-\gamma}\gamma^{\kappa+1}. |
|
Proof.
The proof follows from that of Proposition 1 by augmenting the state space of each agent by 𝒮i×Ωi∋(𝐬i,𝝎i)\mathcal{S}_{i}\times\Omega_{i}\ni(\mathbf{s}_{i},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}), where the augmented state variable 𝝎i\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i} degenerates to a deterministic state (𝝎i​(t)=𝝎i,∀t\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(t)=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i},\forall t).
∎
E.3.2 Policies
Definition 7 (𝝎\boldsymbol{\omega}-ACR for localized policies).
Given the graphical representation of the networked system model 𝒢\mathscr{G} that is encoded in the binary masks 𝐜\mathbf{c}, the policy ACR 𝐬𝒩i∘\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}} obtained from Algorithm 2 or Algorithm 3, for each agent ii and its 11-hop neighborhood 𝒩i\mathcal{N}_{i}, we recursively define domain-specific ACR ω𝒩i∘\omega^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}: the latent factors 𝝎𝒩i\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}} that either
•
𝝎i,j\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i,j} has a direct link to agent ii’s reward rir_{i}, i.e., 𝝎i,j=𝝎ir\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i,j}=\boldsymbol{\omega}^{r}_{i} and 𝐜i,j𝝎→ri=1\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}\rightarrow r_{i}}_{i,j}=1, or
•
𝝎i′,j\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i^{\prime},j} has an edge to a state component si′,l∈𝐬𝒩i∘s_{i^{\prime},l}\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}, i.e., 𝐜i′,j,l𝝎→s=1\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}\rightarrow s}_{i^{\prime},j,l}=1 and si′,l∈𝐬𝒩i∘s_{i^{\prime},l}\in\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}.
Definition 8 (Approximately compact 𝝎\boldsymbol{\omega}-conditioned policy).
A domain-specific localized policy π=(π1,…,πn)\pi=\left(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}\right) is called an approximately compact localized policy if the following holds for all i∈𝒩,𝐬𝒩i,𝐬𝒩i′∈𝒮𝒩i,𝝎𝒩i,𝝎𝒩i′i\in\mathcal{N},\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}:
|
πi(⋅∣𝐬𝒩i∘,𝐬𝒩i/𝐬𝒩i∘,𝝎𝒩i∘,𝝎𝒩i/𝝎𝒩i∘)=πi(⋅∣𝐬𝒩i∘,𝐬𝒩i′/𝐬𝒩i∘,𝝎𝒩i∘,𝝎𝒩i′/𝝎𝒩i∘).\pi_{i}\left(\cdot\mid\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}/\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}/\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{\circ}\right)=\pi_{i}\left(\cdot\mid\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}/\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}/\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{\circ}\right). |
|
In other words, there exists π~i:𝒮𝒩i∘×Ω𝒩i∘↦Δ𝒜i\tilde{\pi}_{i}:\mathcal{S}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}\times\Omega^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}\mapsto\Delta_{\mathcal{A}_{i}} such that πi(⋅∣𝐬𝒩i∘,𝐬𝒩i/𝐬𝒩i∘,𝝎𝒩i∘,𝝎𝒩i/𝝎𝒩i∘)=π~i(⋅∣𝐬𝒩i∘,𝝎𝒩i∘)\pi_{i}\left(\cdot\mid\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}/\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}/\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{\circ}\right)=\tilde{\pi}_{i}\left(\cdot\mid\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{\circ}\right). For convenience, we fix an arbitrary 𝐬𝒩i/𝐬𝒩i∘,𝝎𝒩i/𝝎𝒩i∘\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}/\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}/\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{\circ}, and denote by π~i(⋅∣𝐬𝒩i∘,𝝎𝒩i∘):=πi(⋅∣𝐬𝒩i∘,𝐬𝒩i/𝐬𝒩i∘,𝝎𝒩i∘,𝝎𝒩i/𝝎𝒩i∘)\tilde{\pi}_{i}\left(\cdot\mid\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{\circ}\right):=\pi_{i}\left(\cdot\mid\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}/\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}/\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}^{\circ}\right).
Proposition 9 (Compactness of cross-domain policy ACR).
Let π\pi be an arbitrary localized policy and π~\tilde{\pi} be the corresponding approximately compact localized policy (Definition 8), for each agent ii, and any (𝐬,𝐚,𝝎)∈𝒮×𝒜×Ω(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{\omega})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\Omega, we have
|
Qiπ~​(𝐬,𝐚,𝝎)=Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚,𝝎).Q_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{\omega})=Q_{i}^{\pi}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{\omega}). |
|
Proof.
The proof follows from that of Proposition 6 by augmenting the state space of each agent by 𝒮i×Ωi∋(𝐬i,𝝎i)\mathcal{S}_{i}\times\Omega_{i}\ni(\mathbf{s}_{i},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}), where the augmented state variable 𝝎i\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i} degenerates to a deterministic state (𝝎i​(t)=𝝎i,∀t\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(t)=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i},\forall t).
∎
Corollary 2.
Let π\pi be an arbitrary localized policy and π~\tilde{\pi} be the corresponding approximately compact localized policy output by Algorithm 2, for each agent ii, and any (𝐬,𝐚,𝝎)∈𝒮×𝒜×Ω(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{\omega})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\Omega, the approximation error between Q~iπ~​(𝐬𝒩iκ∘,𝐚𝒩iκ,𝝎𝒩iκ∘)\tilde{Q}_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}}^{\circ}\right) and Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚)Q_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\right) can be bounded as
|
sup(𝐬,𝐚)∈𝒮×𝒜|Q~iπ~​(𝐬𝒩iκ∘,𝐚𝒩iκ,𝝎𝒩iκ∘)−Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚,𝝎)|≤2​r¯1−γ​γκ+1.\displaystyle\sup_{(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left\lvert\tilde{Q}_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}}^{\circ}\right)-Q_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)\right\rvert\leq\frac{2\bar{r}}{1-\gamma}\gamma^{\kappa+1}. |
|
Proof.
The proof follows from that of Corollary 1 by augmenting the state space of each agent by 𝒮i×Ωi∋(𝐬i,𝝎i)\mathcal{S}_{i}\times\Omega_{i}\ni(\mathbf{s}_{i},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}), where the augmented state variable 𝝎i\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i} degenerates to a deterministic state (𝝎i​(t)=𝝎i,∀t\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(t)=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i},\forall t).
∎
Proposition 10 (Approximation error).
Let π\pi be an arbitrary localized policy and π~\tilde{\pi} be the corresponding approximately compact localized policy output by Algorithm 3, for each agent ii, and any (𝐬,𝐚,𝝎)∈𝒮×𝒜×Ω(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{\omega})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\Omega, we have
|
sup(𝐬,𝐚)∈𝒮×𝒜|Qiπ~​(𝐬,𝐚,𝝎)−Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚,𝝎)|≤r¯1−γ​γκ+1.\displaystyle\sup_{(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left\lvert Q_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)-Q_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)\right\rvert\leq\frac{\bar{r}}{1-\gamma}\gamma^{\kappa+1}. |

Corollary 3.
Let π\pi be an arbitrary localized policy and π~\tilde{\pi} be the corresponding approximately compact localized policy output by Algorithm 3, for each agent ii, and any (𝐬,𝐚,𝝎)∈𝒮×𝒜×Ω(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{\omega})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\Omega, the approximation error between Q~iπ~​(𝐬𝒩iκ∘,𝐚𝒩iκ,𝝎𝒩iκ∘)\tilde{Q}_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}}^{\circ}\right) and Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚)Q_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\right) can be bounded as
|
sup(𝐬,𝐚)∈𝒮×𝒜|Q~iπ~​(𝐬𝒩iκ∘,𝐚𝒩iκ,𝝎𝒩iκ∘)−Qiπ​(𝐬,𝐚,𝝎)|≤3​r¯1−γ​γκ+1.\displaystyle\sup_{(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\left\lvert\tilde{Q}_{i}^{\tilde{\pi}}\left(\mathbf{s}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\kappa}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\mathcal{N}^{\kappa}_{i}}^{\circ}\right)-Q_{i}^{\pi}\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)\right\rvert\leq\frac{3\bar{r}}{1-\gamma}\gamma^{\kappa+1}. |
|
Proof.
The proof follows from that of Corollary 2 by augmenting the state space of each agent by 𝒮i×Ωi∋(𝐬i,𝝎i)\mathcal{S}_{i}\times\Omega_{i}\ni(\mathbf{s}_{i},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}), where the augmented state variable 𝝎i\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i} degenerates to a deterministic state (𝝎i​(t)=𝝎i,∀t\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}(t)=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i},\forall t).

Appendix F Missing proofs in Section 5
F.1 Discussion on assumptions in Proposition 5
Our assumptions in Proposition 5 are standard in causal discovery and theoretical MARL.
•
Faithfulness and sub-Gaussian noise: these are classical and standard requirements to ensure identifiability [17, 9] and finite-sample recovery.
•
Bounded degree: bounded neighborhood degree is natural in networked systems where agents have limited communication range. For example, this holds when each intersection connects to only a few roads, or each wireless node has limited interference range.
•
Full observability: while we currently assume full local observability, the ACR construction can be extended to partially observable settings using learned belief states or latent encoders. This is part of our future work.
•
Lipschitz continuity: this mild smoothness assumption is satisfied by most smooth policies.
Importantly, our algorithm can still be applied when assumptions are violated, only the theoretical guarantees may not hold exactly.
F.2 Proof of Theorem 1
Proof.
We will prove this theorem by analyzing the causal structure from the perspective of each individual agent while accounting for inter-agent influences through the network structure.
For each agent ii, we define the variable set 𝒱i\mathcal{V}_{i} that includes:
•
States of agent ii at time t−1t-1: 𝐬i​(t−1)=(si,1​(t−1),…,si,di​(t−1))\mathbf{s}_{i}(t-1)=(s_{i,1}(t-1),\ldots,s_{i,d_{i}}(t-1))
•
States of neighboring agents at time t−1t-1: 𝐬𝒩i∖i​(t−1)={𝐬j​(t−1):j∈𝒩i∖{i}}\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}\setminus i}(t-1)=\{\mathbf{s}_{j}(t-1):j\in\mathcal{N}_{i}\setminus\{i\}\}
•
States of agent ii at time tt: 𝐬i​(t)=(si,1​(t),…,si,di​(t))\mathbf{s}_{i}(t)=(s_{i,1}(t),\ldots,s_{i,d_{i}}(t))
•
Actions of agent ii at time t−1t-1: 𝐚i​(t−1)\mathbf{a}_{i}(t-1)
We denote by m∈{1,…,M}m\in\{1,\ldots,M\} the domain index, which serves as a surrogate variable for the unobserved change factors 𝝎im\boldsymbol{\omega}^{m}_{i}. Let 𝒢i\mathcal{G}_{i} be the causal graph over variables in 𝒱i∪{k}\mathcal{V}_{i}\cup\{k\}. Our goal is to identify the binary structural matrices 𝐜is→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{s\rightarrow s}, 𝐜ia→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{a\rightarrow s}, and 𝐜i𝝎m→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{m}\rightarrow s} which encode the edges in 𝒢i\mathcal{G}_{i}.
Under the Markov condition and faithfulness assumption, we can apply the following fundamental result from causal discovery:
Lemma 8.
For any two variables Vr,Vs∈𝒱iV_{r},V_{s}\in\mathcal{V}_{i}, they are not adjacent in 𝒢i\mathcal{G}_{i} if and only if they are independent conditional on some subset of {Vt:t≠r,t≠s}∪{m}\{V_{t}:t\neq r,t\neq s\}\cup\{m\}.
This lemma follows from the properties of d-separation in causal Bayesian networks, as established in [10] and [17]. For each agent ii, we can therefore identify the skeleton of 𝒢i\mathcal{G}_{i} by testing conditional independence relationships among the variables in 𝒱i∪{m}\mathcal{V}_{i}\cup\{m\}.
The networked MARL system forms a Dynamic Bayesian Network (DBN), where the temporal structure imposes constraints on the edge directions:
1.
Edges can only go from variables at time t−1t-1 to variables at time tt.
2.
There are no instantaneous causal effects between variables at the same time point.
Therefore, for any edge identified in the skeleton of 𝒢i\mathcal{G}_{i} between a variable at time t−1t-1 and a variable at time tt, the direction is determined by the temporal order. Specifically, for each state component si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) of agent ii, any edge from a state component sl,m​(t−1)s_{l,m}(t-1) (where ll could be ii or any neighbor of ii) to si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) must be directed from sl,m​(t−1)s_{l,m}(t-1) to si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t). Similarly, any edge from the action 𝐚i​(t−1)\mathbf{a}_{i}(t-1) to si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) must be directed from 𝐚i​(t−1)\mathbf{a}_{i}(t-1) to si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t).
Given the directed edges in 𝒢i\mathcal{G}_{i}, we can now identify the structural matrices:
1.
Identification of 𝐜is→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{s\rightarrow s}: For each state component si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) of agent ii, the jj-th row of 𝐜is→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{s\rightarrow s}, denoted by 𝐜i,js→s\mathbf{c}_{i,j}^{s\rightarrow s}, is a binary vector where entry mm is 1 if and only if there is a directed edge from s𝒩i,m​(t−1)s_{\mathcal{N}_{i},m}(t-1) to si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) in 𝒢i\mathcal{G}_{i}.
2.
Identification of 𝐜ia→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{a\rightarrow s}: For each state component si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) of agent ii, the entry jj of 𝐜ia→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{a\rightarrow s} is 1 if and only if there is a directed edge from 𝐚i​(t−1)\mathbf{a}_{i}(t-1) to si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) in 𝒢i\mathcal{G}_{i}.
To identify 𝐜i𝝎m→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{m}\rightarrow s}, we need to determine which state components have distribution changes across domains.
Lemma 9.
For a variable Vr∈𝒱iV_{r}\in\mathcal{V}_{i}, its distribution module changes across domains if and only if VrV_{r} and mm are not independent given any subset of other variables in 𝒱i\mathcal{V}_{i}.
This follows from the definition of the domain-specific changes: if a variable’s distribution remains invariant across domains, then it is independent of the domain index mm conditional on its parents. For each state component si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t), we test whether si,j​(t)⟂⟂m|𝒵s_{i,j}(t)\perp\!\!\!\perp m|\mathcal{Z} for any subset 𝒵⊆𝒱i∖{si,j​(t)}\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{i}\setminus\{s_{i,j}(t)\}. If no such conditional independence holds, then the distribution of si,j​(t)s_{i,j}(t) varies across domains, and therefore 𝐜i,j𝝎m→s\mathbf{c}_{i,j}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{m}\rightarrow s} has at least one non-zero entry.
To identify the specific entries of 𝐜i𝝎m→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{m}\rightarrow s}, we exploit the fact that changes in distributions are localized according to the causal structure.
To complete the proof, we need to show that the structural matrices are uniquely identified from the observed data:
1.
Uniqueness of 𝐜is→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{s\rightarrow s} and 𝐜ia→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{a\rightarrow s}: These matrices encode the causal connections in the DBN structure. Under the Markov and faithfulness assumptions, the skeleton of the DBN is uniquely identified. Combined with the temporal constraints on edge directions, the matrices 𝐜is→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{s\rightarrow s} and 𝐜ia→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{a\rightarrow s} are uniquely determined.
2.
Uniqueness of 𝐜iωm→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{m}\rightarrow s}: This matrix encodes which state components have distribution changes across domains. Under the faithfulness assumption, a state component’s distribution changes across domains if and only if it is not conditionally independent of the domain index mm given any subset of other variables. This provides a unique characterization of the non-zero entries in 𝐜i𝝎m→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{m}\rightarrow s}.
A critical aspect of our proof is that we treat each agent’s local model separately while accounting for the influence of neighboring agents through their observed states. This is justified by the factorization of the joint transition model:
|
P⁡(𝐬⁡(t+1)|𝐬⁡(t),𝐚⁡(t))=∏i=1nPi​(𝐬i​(t+1)|𝐬𝒩i​(t),𝐚i​(t)).P(\mathbf{s}(t+1)|\mathbf{s}(t),\mathbf{a}(t))=\prod_{i=1}^{n}P_{i}(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t+1)|\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t)). |
|
This factorization ensures that, for each agent ii, we only need to consider the states of its neighbors 𝒩i\mathcal{N}_{i} to fully capture the relevant causal influences.
Therefore, under the Markov condition and faithfulness assumption, the structural matrices 𝐜is→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{s\rightarrow s}, 𝐜ia→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{a\rightarrow s}, and 𝐜i𝝎m→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{m}\rightarrow s} are identifiable for each agent ii in the networked MARL system.
∎
F.3 Proof of Proposition 4
Proposition 11 (Formal statement of proposition 4).
Suppose the following conditions hold: (i) the Markov condition and faithfulness assumption are satisfied for the causal graph 𝒢i\mathcal{G}_{i} of each agent ii, (ii) For each true causal edge (X→Y)(X\rightarrow Y) in 𝒢i\mathcal{G}_{i}, the minimal conditional mutual information is lower-bounded: I⁡(X;Y|Z)≥λ>0I(X;Y|Z)\geq\lambda>0 for any conditioning set ZZ that d-separates XX and YY in 𝒢i∖{X→Y}\mathcal{G}_{i}\setminus\{X\rightarrow Y\}, (iii) The maximal in-degree of any node in 𝒢i\mathcal{G}_{i} is bounded by dmaxd_{\max}, (iv) The noise terms ϵi,js​(t)\epsilon_{i,j}^{s}(t) are sub-Gaussian with parameter σ2\sigma^{2}, (v) Each element of 𝐬𝒩i​(t)\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t) and 𝐚i​(t)\mathbf{a}_{i}(t) has bounded magnitude by BB, (vi) The transition functions fi,jf_{i,j} are LfL_{f}-Lipschitz continuous in all arguments. Then, with probability at least 1−δ1-\delta, the estimated causal masks 𝐂^is→s\hat{\mathbf{C}}_{i}^{s\rightarrow s}, 𝐜^ia→s\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{i}^{a\rightarrow s}, and 𝐂^iω→s\hat{\mathbf{C}}_{i}^{\omega\rightarrow s} will match the true causal masks for
|
Te=Ω⁡(di⋅dmax⋅log⁡(di⋅n/δ)λ2),T_{e}=\Omega\left(\frac{d_{i}\cdot d_{\max}\cdot\log(d_{i}\cdot n/\delta)}{\lambda^{2}}\right), |
|
where di=dim(𝐬𝒩i)d_{i}=\dim(\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}) is the total dimension of the state space for agent ii and its neighbors.
Proof.
The proof consists of four main parts: (1) formulating the statistical estimation problem, (2) analyzing the error in conditional independence tests, (3) bounding the error probability in structure learning, and (4) deriving the sample complexity.
Step 1: Formulation of the statistical estimation problem. For each agent ii, we need to estimate the causal graph 𝒢i\mathcal{G}_{i} from observed trajectories {(𝐬𝒩i​(t),𝐚i​(t),𝐬i​(t+1))}t=0Te−1\{(\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t),\mathbf{s}_{i}(t+1))\}_{t=0}^{T_{e}-1}.
Define the variable set 𝒱i\mathcal{V}_{i} for agent ii that includes:
•
States of agent ii at time t−1t-1: 𝐬i​(t−1)\mathbf{s}_{i}(t-1)
•
States of neighboring agents at time t−1t-1: 𝐬𝒩i∖i​(t−1)\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}\setminus i}(t-1)
•
States of agent ii at time tt: 𝐬i​(t)\mathbf{s}_{i}(t)
•
Actions of agent ii at time t−1t-1: 𝐚i​(t−1)\mathbf{a}_{i}(t-1)
•
Domain index kk, which serves as a surrogate for the unobserved change factors 𝝎ik\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}^{k}
We employ a constraint-based causal discovery approach, which identifies the causal structure by performing a series of conditional independence tests based on the key insight from Lemma 13:
Lemma 10.
For any two variables Vr,Vs∈𝒱iV_{r},V_{s}\in\mathcal{V}_{i}, they are not adjacent in 𝒢i\mathcal{G}_{i} if and only if they are independent conditional on some subset of {Vt:t≠r,t≠s}∪{m}\{V_{t}:t\neq r,t\neq s\}\cup\{m\}.
Step 2: Analysis of conditional independence tests. For analyzing conditional independence, we use the mutual information measure. For variables XX and YY conditional on set ZZ, the conditional mutual information is:
|
I⁡(X;Y|Z)=𝔼X,Y,Z⁡[log⁡P⁡(X,Y|Z)P⁡(X|Z)​P​(Y|Z)].I(X;Y|Z)=\E_{X,Y,Z}\left[\log\frac{P(X,Y|Z)}{P(X|Z)P(Y|Z)}\right]. |
|
In practice, we estimate this from data using the empirical distributions:
|
I^​(X;Y|Z)=∑x,y,zP^​(x,y,z)​log⁡P^​(x,y,z)​P^​(z)P^​(x,z)​P^​(y,z),\hat{I}(X;Y|Z)=\sum_{x,y,z}\hat{P}(x,y,z)\log\frac{\hat{P}(x,y,z)\hat{P}(z)}{\hat{P}(x,z)\hat{P}(y,z)}, |
|
where P^\hat{P} denotes empirical probabilities based on the observed data.
The estimation error can be bounded using concentration inequalities. For any ϵ>0\epsilon>0:
|
P⁡(|I^​(X;Y|Z)−I⁡(X;Y|Z)|>ϵ)≤2​exp⁡(−Te​ϵ2C1​|𝒳​‖𝒴‖​𝒵|),P(|\hat{I}(X;Y|Z)-I(X;Y|Z)|>\epsilon)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{T_{e}\epsilon^{2}}{C_{1}|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}||\mathcal{Z}|}\right), |
|
where |𝒳||\mathcal{X}|, |𝒴||\mathcal{Y}|, and |𝒵||\mathcal{Z}| are the cardinalities of the respective variable domains, and C1C_{1} is a universal constant.
For continuous variables, we can use kernel-based estimators or discretization. With appropriate binning of continuous variables, the error bound becomes:
|
P⁡(|I^​(X;Y|Z)−I⁡(X;Y|Z)|>ϵ)≤2​exp⁡(−Te​ϵ2C2​bX​bY​bZ​log2⁡(Te)),P(|\hat{I}(X;Y|Z)-I(X;Y|Z)|>\epsilon)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{T_{e}\epsilon^{2}}{C_{2}b_{X}b_{Y}b_{Z}\log^{2}(T_{e})}\right), |
|
where bXb_{X}, bYb_{Y}, and bZb_{Z} are the number of bins used for each variable, and C2C_{2} is a constant. The log2⁡(Te)\log^{2}(T_{e}) term arises from the adaptive discretization of continuous variables.
Step 3: bounding the error probability in structure learning. Let 𝒢i\mathcal{G}_{i} be the true causal graph for agent ii, and 𝒢^i\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{i} be the estimated graph. We need to bound P⁡(𝒢^i≠𝒢i)P(\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{i}\neq\mathcal{G}_{i}).
The structure learning error can occur in two ways:
1.
Missing a true edge (Type II error in a conditional independence test)
2.
Adding a spurious edge (Type I error in a conditional independence test)
For Type I errors, we set the significance level to α=δ2​M\alpha=\frac{\delta}{2M}, where MM is the total number of conditional independence tests performed. For Type II errors, we use the fact that when variables are dependent with conditional mutual information at least γ\gamma, the probability of wrongly concluding independence is:
|
P⁡(Type II error)≤exp⁡(−Te​γ2C3​|𝒳​‖𝒴‖​𝒵|).P(\text{Type II error})\leq\exp\left(-\frac{T_{e}\gamma^{2}}{C_{3}|\mathcal{X}||\mathcal{Y}||\mathcal{Z}|}\right). |
|
where C3C_{3} is a constant that depends on the distribution properties.
The total number of conditional independence tests is bounded by M≤di2⋅∑j=0dmax(di−2j)≤di2⋅(didmax)≤di2⋅(e​di/dmax)dmaxM\leq d_{i}^{2}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{d_{\max}}\binom{d_{i}-2}{j}\leq d_{i}^{2}\cdot\binom{d_{i}}{d_{\max}}\leq d_{i}^{2}\cdot(ed_{i}/d_{\max})^{d_{\max}}.
By the union bound, the probability of any error in structure learning is:
|
P⁡(𝒢^i≠𝒢i)≤M⋅max⁡{α,exp⁡(−Te​γ2C3​dmax)}.P(\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{i}\neq\mathcal{G}_{i})\leq M\cdot\max\left\{\alpha,\exp\left(-\frac{T_{e}\gamma^{2}}{C_{3}d_{\max}}\right)\right\}. |
|
where we’ve used that the maximum size of any conditioning set is dmaxd_{\max}, and the maximum cardinality of any variable domain is bounded by a function of dmaxd_{\max} given our discretization scheme.
Step 4: deriving the sample complexity. To ensure P⁡(𝒢^i≠𝒢i)≤δP(\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{i}\neq\mathcal{G}_{i})\leq\delta, we need:
1.
α=δ2​M≤δ2​di2⋅(e​di/dmax)dmax\alpha=\frac{\delta}{2M}\leq\frac{\delta}{2d_{i}^{2}\cdot(ed_{i}/d_{\max})^{d_{\max}}}.
2.
exp⁡(−Te​γ2C3​dmax)≤δ2​M≤δ2​di2⋅(e​di/dmax)dmax\exp\left(-\frac{T_{e}\gamma^{2}}{C_{3}d_{\max}}\right)\leq\frac{\delta}{2M}\leq\frac{\delta}{2d_{i}^{2}\cdot(ed_{i}/d_{\max})^{d_{\max}}}.
From the second condition, we derive:
|
Te​γ2C3​dmax≥log⁡(2​di2⋅(e​di/dmax)dmaxδ).\frac{T_{e}\gamma^{2}}{C_{3}d_{\max}}\geq\log\left(\frac{2d_{i}^{2}\cdot(ed_{i}/d_{\max})^{d_{\max}}}{\delta}\right). |
|
Therefore:
|
Te≥C3​dmaxγ2​log⁡(2​di2⋅(e​di/dmax)dmaxδ).T_{e}\geq\frac{C_{3}d_{\max}}{\gamma^{2}}\log\left(\frac{2d_{i}^{2}\cdot(ed_{i}/d_{\max})^{d_{\max}}}{\delta}\right). |
|
Now, we need to analyze this expression more carefully:
|
log⁡(2​di2⋅(e​di/dmax)dmaxδ)=log⁡(2​di2δ)+dmax​log⁡(e​didmax).\log\left(\frac{2d_{i}^{2}\cdot(ed_{i}/d_{\max})^{d_{\max}}}{\delta}\right)=\log\left(\frac{2d_{i}^{2}}{\delta}\right)+d_{\max}\log\left(\frac{ed_{i}}{d_{\max}}\right). |
|
Under the sparsity assumption (dmax≪did_{\max}\ll d_{i}), we have:
|
dmax​log⁡(e​didmax)≤dmax​log⁡(e​di)=O⁡(dmax​log⁡(di)).d_{\max}\log\left(\frac{ed_{i}}{d_{\max}}\right)\leq d_{\max}\log(ed_{i})=O(d_{\max}\log(d_{i})). |
|
Therefore:
|
Te≥C3​dmaxγ2​[log⁡(2​di2δ)+O⁡(dmax​log⁡(di))].T_{e}\geq\frac{C_{3}d_{\max}}{\gamma^{2}}\left[\log\left(\frac{2d_{i}^{2}}{\delta}\right)+O(d_{\max}\log(d_{i}))\right]. |
|
This simplifies to:
|
Te=O⁡(dmax2​log⁡(di)+dmax​log⁡(1/δ)γ2).T_{e}=O\left(\frac{d_{\max}^{2}\log(d_{i})+d_{\max}\log(1/\delta)}{\gamma^{2}}\right). |
|
For most practical scenarios where dmax=O⁡(log⁡(di))d_{\max}=O(\log(d_{i})) (very sparse graphs), this further simplifies to:
|
Te=O⁡(dmax​log⁡(di)​log⁡(di/δ)γ2).T_{e}=O\left(\frac{d_{\max}\log(d_{i})\log(d_{i}/\delta)}{\gamma^{2}}\right). |
|
However, for a more general bound without assuming extreme sparsity, we have:
|
Te=O⁡(dmax2​log⁡(di)+dmax​log⁡(1/δ)γ2).T_{e}=O\left(\frac{d_{\max}^{2}\log(d_{i})+d_{\max}\log(1/\delta)}{\gamma^{2}}\right). |
|
Now, accounting for the fact that we’re learning causal masks for all nn agents, and applying a union bound across agents, we replace δ\delta with δ/n\delta/n, yielding:
|
Te=O⁡(dmax2​log⁡(di)+dmax​log⁡(n/δ)γ2).T_{e}=O\left(\frac{d_{\max}^{2}\log(d_{i})+d_{\max}\log(n/\delta)}{\gamma^{2}}\right). |
|
For typical networked systems where did_{i} scales with the number of neighbors, this gives us our final sample complexity:
|
Te=Ω⁡(di⋅dmax⋅log⁡(di⋅n/δ)γ2).T_{e}=\Omega\left(\frac{d_{i}\cdot d_{\max}\cdot\log(d_{i}\cdot n/\delta)}{\gamma^{2}}\right). |
|
∎
F.4 Proof of Proposition 5
Proposition 12 (Formal statement of Proposition 5).
Suppose the following conditions hold: (i) The true causal masks are estimated accurately, (ii) the transition dynamics are identifiable with respect to the domain factor, i.e., for any two distinct domain factors 𝝎i≠𝝎i′\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}\neq\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}^{\prime}, there exists a set of states and actions with non-zero measure such that the conditional distributions P⁡(𝐬i,j​(t+1)|𝐬𝒩i​(t),𝐚i​(t),𝝎i)P(\mathbf{s}_{i,j}(t+1)|\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t),\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}) and P⁡(si,j​(t+1)|𝐬𝒩i​(t),𝐚i​(t),𝝎i′)P(s_{i,j}(t+1)|\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t),\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}^{\prime}) are distinguishable. (iii) The noise terms ϵi,js​(t)\epsilon_{i,j}^{s}(t) are independent, sub-Gaussian with parameter σ2\sigma^{2}. (iv) The domain factor 𝝎im∈Ωi\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}^{m}\in\Omega_{i} belongs to a compact set with diameter DΩD_{\Omega}. (v) For any two distinct domain factors 𝝎i≠𝝎i′\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}\neq\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}^{\prime} with ‖𝝎i−𝝎i′‖2≥ϵ\|\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}^{\prime}\|_{2}\geq\epsilon,
|
‖P⁡(𝐬i​(t+1)|𝐬𝒩i​(t),𝐚i​(t),𝝎i)−P⁡(𝐬i​(t+1)|𝐬𝒩i​(t),𝐚i​(t),𝝎i′)‖TV≥α​ϵ\|P(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t+1)|\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t),\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i})-P(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t+1)|\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t),\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i}^{\prime})\|_{\text{TV}}\geq\alpha\epsilon |
|
for some constant α>0\alpha>0. Then, for any domain with true domain factor 𝝎∗\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}, given a trajectory of length TeT_{e}, the estimated domain factor 𝝎^\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}} satisfies:
|
‖𝝎^−𝝎∗‖2≤C𝝎​DΩ​log⁡(n​Te/δ)Te\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}\|_{2}\leq C_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\sqrt{\frac{D_{\Omega}\log(nT_{e}/\delta)}{T_{e}}} |
|
with probability at least 1−δ1-\delta, where DΩD_{\Omega} is the dimension of the domain factor space, and C𝝎C_{\boldsymbol{\omega}} is a constant that depends on LfL_{f}, σ\sigma, α\alpha, and the conditioning of the estimation problem.
Proof.
The proof consists of three main parts: (1) formulating the domain factor estimation problem, (2) analyzing the estimation error using statistical learning theory, and (3) deriving the final sample complexity bound.
Step 1: formulation of the domain factor estimation problem. Given the known causal structure encoded by masks 𝐂is→s\mathbf{C}_{i}^{s\rightarrow s}, 𝐜ia→s\mathbf{c}_{i}^{a\rightarrow s}, and 𝐂iω→s\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\omega\rightarrow s}, the domain factor estimation problem can be formulated as finding the domain factor 𝝎\boldsymbol{\omega} that best explains the observed transitions.
For agent ii, we have observed trajectories {(𝐬𝒩i​(t),𝐚i​(t),𝐬i​(t+1))}t=0Te−1\{(\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t),\mathbf{s}_{i}(t+1))\}_{t=0}^{T_{e}-1} generated from the true domain factor 𝝎∗\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}. We can define a negative log-likelihood function for each agent ii:
|
ℒi(𝝎)=−1Te∑t=0Te−1logP(𝐬i(t+1)|𝐬𝒩i(t),𝐚i(t),𝝎).\mathcal{L}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\omega})=-\frac{1}{T_{e}}\sum_{t=0}^{T_{e}-1}\log P(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t+1)|\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t),\boldsymbol{\omega}). |
|
The maximum likelihood estimator (MLE) for the domain factor is:
|
𝝎^=arg⁡min𝝎∈Ω​1n​∑i=1nℒi​(𝝎).\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}=\arg\min_{\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\Omega}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{L}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\omega}). |
|
Step 2: analysis of the estimation error. To analyze the estimation error, we use the theory of M-estimators and the properties of the negative log-likelihood function. First, we establish some key properties of the negative log-likelihood.
Lemma 11 (Lipschitz continuity of negative log-likelihood).
Under the assumption that the transition function fi,jf_{i,j} is LfL_{f}-Lipschitz continuous with respect to all arguments, the negative log-likelihood function ℒi​(𝝎)\mathcal{L}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\omega}) is LℒL_{\mathcal{L}}-Lipschitz continuous with respect to 𝝎\boldsymbol{\omega}, where Lℒ=O⁡(Lf/σ2)L_{\mathcal{L}}=O(L_{f}/\sigma^{2}).
Proof.
For Gaussian noise with variance σ2\sigma^{2}, the conditional probability density is:
|
|
P⁡(𝐬i​(t+1)|𝐬𝒩i​(t),𝐚i​(t),𝝎)\displaystyle P(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t+1)|\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t),\boldsymbol{\omega}) |
|
|
|
=∏j=1di12​π​σ2​exp⁡(−(si,j​(t+1)−fi,j​(𝐜𝒩i,js→s⊙𝐬𝒩i​(t),𝐜i,ja→s⋅𝐚i​(t),𝐜i,jω→s⊙𝝎,0))22​σ2).\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{d_{i}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{(s_{i,j}(t+1)-f_{i,j}(\mathbf{c}_{\mathcal{N}_{i},j}^{s\rightarrow s}\odot\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t),\mathbf{c}_{i,j}^{a\rightarrow s}\cdot\mathbf{a}_{i}(t),\mathbf{c}_{i,j}^{\omega\rightarrow s}\odot\boldsymbol{\omega},0))^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right). |
|
Taking the negative logarithm:
|
|
−log⁡P⁡(𝐬i​(t+1)|𝐬𝒩i​(t),𝐚i​(t),𝝎)\displaystyle-\log P(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t+1)|\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t),\boldsymbol{\omega}) |
|
|
|
=∑j=1di[12​log⁡(2​π​σ2)+(si,j​(t+1)−fi,j​(𝐜𝒩i,js→s⊙𝐬𝒩i​(t),𝐜i,ja→s⋅𝐚i​(t),𝐜i,jω→s⊙𝝎,0))22​σ2].\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{i}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^{2})+\frac{(s_{i,j}(t+1)-f_{i,j}(\mathbf{c}_{\mathcal{N}_{i},j}^{s\rightarrow s}\odot\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t),\mathbf{c}_{i,j}^{a\rightarrow s}\cdot\mathbf{a}_{i}(t),\mathbf{c}_{i,j}^{\omega\rightarrow s}\odot\boldsymbol{\omega},0))^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right]. |
|
The gradient with respect to 𝝎\boldsymbol{\omega} is:
|
∇𝝎[−logP(𝐬i(t+1)|𝐬𝒩i(t),𝐚i(t),𝝎)]=−∑j=1di(si,j​(t+1)−fi,j)σ2∇𝝎fi,j.\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}[-\log P(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t+1)|\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t),\boldsymbol{\omega})]=-\sum_{j=1}^{d_{i}}\frac{(s_{i,j}(t+1)-f_{i,j})}{\sigma^{2}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}f_{i,j}. |
|
Since fi,jf_{i,j} is LfL_{f}-Lipschitz and ‖∇𝝎fi,j‖≤Lf​‖𝐜i,jω→s‖\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}f_{i,j}\|\leq L_{f}\|\mathbf{c}_{i,j}^{\omega\rightarrow s}\|, we have:
|
‖∇𝝎[−log⁡P⁡(𝐬i​(t+1)|𝐬𝒩i​(t),𝐚i​(t),𝝎)]‖≤di​Lf​Bσ2=𝒪⁡(Lfσ2).\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}[-\log P(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t+1)|\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t),\boldsymbol{\omega})]\|\leq\frac{d_{i}L_{f}B}{\sigma^{2}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{L_{f}}{\sigma^{2}}\right). |
|
Therefore, ℒi​(𝝎)\mathcal{L}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\omega}) is LℒL_{\mathcal{L}}-Lipschitz continuous with Lℒ=𝒪⁡(Lf/σ2)L_{\mathcal{L}}=\mathcal{O}(L_{f}/\sigma^{2}).
∎
Lemma 12 (Strong convexity of expected negative log-likelihood).
Under the identifiability assumption and the total variation distance condition, the expected negative log-likelihood 𝔼⁡[ℒi​(𝝎)]\E[\mathcal{L}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\omega})] is μ\mu-strongly convex in a neighborhood of the true domain factor 𝝎∗\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}, where μ\mu depends on α\alpha and the minimum eigenvalue of the Fisher information matrix.
Proof.
The expected negative log-likelihood is:
|
𝔼⁡[ℒi​(𝝎)]=−𝔼𝐬𝒩i,𝐚i,𝐬i​(t+1)⁡[log⁡P⁡(𝐬i​(t+1)|𝐬𝒩i​(t),𝐚i​(t),𝝎)].\E[\mathcal{L}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\omega})]=-\E_{\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{s}_{i}(t+1)}[\log P(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t+1)|\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}(t),\mathbf{a}_{i}(t),\boldsymbol{\omega})]. |
|
The Hessian matrix of this function is the Fisher information matrix:
|
H⁡(𝝎)=𝔼𝐬𝒩i,𝐚i⁡[𝔼𝐬i​(t+1)|𝐬𝒩i,𝐚i,𝝎∗⁡[∇𝝎​log​P​(𝐬i​(t+1)|𝐬𝒩i,𝐚i,𝝎)​∇𝝎​log⁡P​(𝐬i​(t+1)|𝐬𝒩i,𝐚i,𝝎)T]].H(\boldsymbol{\omega})=\E_{\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{i}}\left[\E_{\mathbf{s}_{i}(t+1)|\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{i},\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}}\left[\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\log P(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t+1)|\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{i},\boldsymbol{\omega})\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\log P(\mathbf{s}_{i}(t+1)|\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}},\mathbf{a}_{i},\boldsymbol{\omega})^{T}\right]\right]. |
|
By the identifiability assumption and the total variation distance condition, for any unit vector vv, we have:
|
vT​H​(𝝎∗)​v≥α2>0.v^{T}H(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*})v\geq\alpha^{2}>0. |
|
Therefore, in a neighborhood of 𝝎∗\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}, the expected negative log-likelihood is μ\mu-strongly convex with μ≥α2\mu\geq\alpha^{2}.
∎
Now, we can analyze the estimation error using concentration inequalities for empirical processes. Let:
|
ℒ⁡(𝝎)=1n​∑i=1nℒi​(𝝎).\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{L}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\omega}). |
|
Proposition 13 (Uniform convergence).
With probability at least 1−δ1-\delta, for all 𝝎∈Ω\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\Omega:
|
|ℒ⁡(𝝎)−𝔼⁡[ℒ⁡(𝝎)]|≤C​DΩ​log⁡(Te/δ)Te,|\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})-\E[\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})]|\leq C\sqrt{\frac{D_{\Omega}\log(T_{e}/\delta)}{T_{e}}}, |
|
where CC is a constant depending on LℒL_{\mathcal{L}} and DΩD_{\Omega}.
Proof.
Since ℒi​(𝝎)\mathcal{L}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\omega}) is LℒL_{\mathcal{L}}-Lipschitz continuous, we can use covering number arguments. The ϵ\epsilon-covering number of the domain factor space Ω\Omega with diameter DΩD_{\Omega} is N⁡(Ω,ϵ)≤(1+DΩϵ)DΩN(\Omega,\epsilon)\leq(1+\frac{D_{\Omega}}{\epsilon})^{D_{\Omega}}.
By the union bound over an ϵ\epsilon-net and Hoeffding’s inequality, we have:
|
P⁡(sup𝝎∈Ω|ℒ⁡(𝝎)−𝔼⁡[ℒ⁡(𝝎)]|>ϵ)≤2​N​(Ω,ϵ2​Lℒ)​exp⁡(−2​Te​ϵ2Bℒ2).P\left(\sup_{\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\Omega}|\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})-\E[\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})]|>\epsilon\right)\leq 2N(\Omega,\frac{\epsilon}{2L_{\mathcal{L}}})\exp\left(-\frac{2T_{e}\epsilon^{2}}{B_{\mathcal{L}}^{2}}\right). |
|
where BℒB_{\mathcal{L}} is the bound on the negative log-likelihood function. Setting the right-hand side to δ\delta and solving for ϵ\epsilon, we get:
|
ϵ=𝒪⁡(DΩ​log⁡(Te/δ)Te).\epsilon=\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{\frac{D_{\Omega}\log(T_{e}/\delta)}{T_{e}}}\right). |
|
∎
Step 3: Deriving the sample complexity bound. By combining the strong convexity of 𝔼⁡[ℒ⁡(𝝎)]\E[\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})] and the uniform convergence bound, we can derive the estimation error. For the true domain factor 𝝎∗\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*} and the estimated domain factor 𝝎^\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}, we have:
|
ℒ⁡(𝝎^)≤ℒ⁡(𝝎∗).\mathcal{L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}})\leq\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}). |
|
This implies:
|
𝔼⁡[ℒ⁡(𝝎^)]\displaystyle\E[\mathcal{L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}})] |
≤ℒ⁡(𝝎^)+ϵ≤ℒ⁡(𝝎∗)+ϵ≤𝔼⁡[ℒ⁡(𝝎∗)]+2​ϵ.\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}})+\epsilon\leq\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*})+\epsilon\leq\E[\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*})]+2\epsilon. |
|
By the strong convexity of 𝔼⁡[ℒ⁡(𝝎)]\E[\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})], we have:
|
𝔼⁡[ℒ⁡(𝝎^)]−𝔼⁡[ℒ⁡(𝝎∗)]≥μ2​‖𝝎^−𝝎∗‖22.\E[\mathcal{L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}})]-\E[\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*})]\geq\frac{\mu}{2}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}. |
|
Combining these inequalities:
|
μ2​‖𝝎^−𝝎∗‖22≤2​ϵ=O⁡(DΩ​log⁡(Te/δ)Te).\frac{\mu}{2}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\epsilon=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{D_{\Omega}\log(T_{e}/\delta)}{T_{e}}}\right). |
|
Therefore:
|
‖𝝎^−𝝎∗‖2≤O⁡(DΩ​log⁡(Te/δ)μ​Te).\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}\|_{2}\leq O\left(\sqrt{\frac{D_{\Omega}\log(T_{e}/\delta)}{\mu T_{e}}}\right). |
|
Now, accounting for the fact that we have nn agents, we apply a union bound across all agents by replacing δ\delta with δ/n\delta/n. This gives us:
|
‖𝝎^−𝝎∗‖2≤C𝝎​DΩ​log⁡(n​Te/δ)Te.\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}\|_{2}\leq C_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\sqrt{\frac{D_{\Omega}\log(nT_{e}/\delta)}{T_{e}}}. |
|
with probability at least 1−δ1-\delta, where C𝝎=O⁡(1/μ)=O⁡(1/α)C_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}=O(1/\sqrt{\mu})=O(1/\alpha) is a constant depending on the problem parameters. This completes the proof of the theorem, providing a rigorous guarantee on the accuracy of domain factor estimation based on the trajectory length TeT_{e}.
∎
Appendix G Missing proofs in Section 6
G.1 Discussions on Assumption 1-6
While Assumption 1-6 are standard for establishing theoretical guarantees, we discuss whether they hold exactly in practice as follows.
Finite MDP (Assumption 1): in some systems, the state or action spaces might not finite or continuous. For such cases, our results can still extend by applying state aggregation or clipping methods to ensure boundedness while incurring small approximation error.
Mixing properties (Assumption 2): real-world networked systems may exhibit slow mixing or non-Lipschitz dynamics. Our theoretical results would degrade gracefully, leading to larger constants τ\tau in the sample complexity bounds.
Smoothness (Assumption 3): the assumption requires the policy gradients ∇θi​log​πi\nabla_{\theta_{i}}\log\pi_{i} to be Lipschitz and uniformly bounded. This holds for all commonly used policy parameterizations (e.g., softmax policies) with bounded inputs and finite-dimensional parameter vectors. It ensures stable policy updates and is common in the convergence analysis of policy gradient and actor-critic algorithms. GSAC uses softmax parameterizations for policies, where log⁡πθi\log\pi_{\theta_{i}} is smooth and has bounded gradients when action probabilities are not close to zero (a condition satisfied by entropy regularization or initialization).
Compact parameter space (Assumption 4): policy parameters θi\theta_{i} are always constrained within a finite range due to neural network weight regularization, weight clipping, or bounded initialization. This is standard in actor-critic methods, where optimization is performed with bounded learning rates and regularizer that implicitly keep the parameters within a compact set.
Smoothness w.r.t. domain factor (Assumption 5): this is reasonable because domain factors, e.g., channel gains, traffic loads, represent physical parameters that vary smoothly. The local dynamics PiP_{i} are typically analytic or polynomial in ωi\omega_{i}, so small perturbations in ωi\omega_{i} induce proportionally small changes in state transitions and rewards. Consequently, the Q-function Qiπ​(s,a,ω)Q^{\pi}_{i}(s,a,\omega) and policy gradient ∇θiJ​(θ,ω)\nabla_{\theta_{i}}J(\theta,\omega) inherit this smoothness. Moreover, standard policy parameterizations and neural network approximators are Lipschitz when combined with bounded weights or gradient clipping. Thus, Assumption 5 aligns well with both the structure of real-world systems and common RL practices. Moreover, even if the true dynamics were not strictly Lipschitz, domain factor estimation (Proposition 5) uses a small-perturbation assumption around ω\omega for adaptation. In practice, state or reward clipping and function approximation, which are inherently Lipschitz, effectively enforce this assumption.
Compact domain factor space (Assumption 6): the latent domain factors ωi\omega_{i} represent bounded variations in environment dynamics (e.g., channel gains, traffic intensities, or reward scaling factors), which are inherently limited by the physical system. In real-world networked systems, these factors are naturally confined to finite ranges determined by system design (e.g., transmission powers, queue lengths). Hence, assuming Ωi\Omega_{i} is compact with bounded diameter DΩD_{\Omega} is both reasonable and practical.
For simplicity, we also write Q𝝎:=Q⁡(⋅,⋅,𝝎)Q^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}:=Q(\cdot,\cdot,\boldsymbol{\omega}) and 𝐳:=(𝐬,𝐚)\mathbf{z}:=(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}).

Proof sketch of Theorem 3.

Bounds on e2​(k)e^{2}(k).
Let 𝒢k\mathcal{G}_{k} be the σ\sigma-algebra generated by the trajectories in the first kk outer-loop iterations. In particular, let 𝒢0\mathcal{G}_{0} be the σ\sigma-algebra generated the trajectories in the first phase of Algorithm 1. Note that 𝝎1:M\boldsymbol{\omega}^{1:M} and 𝝎^1:M\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}^{1:M} are 𝒢0\mathcal{G}_{0}-measurable. In addition, θ⁡(k)\theta(k) is 𝒢k−1\mathcal{G}_{k-1}-measurable, and so is hi​(k)h_{i}(k). Further, by the way that the trajectory {(s⁡(t),a⁡(t))}t=0T\{(s(t),a(t))\}_{t=0}^{T} is generated, we have 𝔼⁡[g⁡(k)|𝒢k−1]=h⁡(k)\mathbb{E}[g(k)|\mathcal{G}_{k-1}]=h(k). As such, ηk​⟨∇J​(θ⁡(k)),e2​(k)⟩\eta_{k}\langle\nabla J(\theta(k)),e^{2}(k)\rangle is a martingale difference sequence w.r.t. 𝒢k\mathcal{G}_{k}, and we have the following bound which is a direct consequence of Azuma-Hoeffding bound.
Lemma 15 (Adapted Lemma 15 [20]).
With probability at least 1−δ/41-\delta/4, we have
|
|∑k=0K−1ηk​⟨∇J​(θ⁡(k)),e2​(k)⟩|≤2​r¯2​L2(1−γ)4​2​∑k=0K−1ηk2​log⁡8δ.\left|\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\eta_{k}\langle\nabla J(\theta(k)),e^{2}(k)\rangle\right|\leq\frac{2\bar{r}^{2}L^{2}}{(1-\gamma)^{4}}\sqrt{2\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\eta_{k}^{2}\log\frac{8}{\delta}}. |

Bounds on e5​(k)e^{5}(k).
We first state the uniform concentration bound for vector-valued functions.
Lemma 17.
Let (x1,x2,…,xM)(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{M}) be i.i.d. samples from a distribution DD on 𝒳\mathcal{X}. Let Θ⊂ℝp\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}, and consider f:𝒳×Θ→ℝdf:\mathcal{X}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{d} with the following properties: (i) For all j=1,…,dj=1,\ldots,d, |fj​(x,θ)|≤B​for all ​(x,θ)|f_{j}(x,\theta)|\leq B\ \text{for all }(x,\theta); (ii) For all x∈𝒳x\in\mathcal{X} and j=1,…,dj=1,\ldots,d, |fj​(x,θ)−fj​(x,θ′)|≤L⋅‖θ−θ′‖2|f_{j}(x,\theta)-f_{j}(x,\theta^{\prime})|\leq L\cdot\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}. Then, with probability at least 1−δ/41-\delta/4,
|
supθ∈Θ‖1M​∑i=1Mf⁡(xi,θ)−𝔼⁡[f⁡(X,θ)]‖2=𝒪⁡((B+L)​d​p​log⁡M+log⁡(4/δ)M),\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left\|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}f(x_{i},\theta)-\mathbb{E}[f(X,\theta)]\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left((B+L)\sqrt{\frac{dp\log M+\log(4/\delta)}{M}}\right), |
|
where the hidden constant is universal.
Observe that for 𝔼⁡[∇θJ​(θ,𝒟M)]=∇θJ​(θ)\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla_{\theta}J(\theta,\mathcal{D}^{M})\right]=\nabla_{\theta}J(\theta). Using Lemma 17, we have with probability at least 1−δ/41-\delta/4,
|
supk‖e5​(k)‖\displaystyle\sup_{k}\left\lVert e^{5}(k)\right\rVert |
≤supk,θ∈Θ‖∇θJ​(θ,𝒟M)−∇θJ​(θ)‖\displaystyle\leq\sup_{k,\theta\in\Theta}\left\lVert\nabla_{\theta}J(\theta,\mathcal{D}^{M})-\nabla_{\theta}J(\theta)\right\rVert |
|

≤𝒪⁡((r¯​L(1−γ)2+Lθ)​dθ​log⁡(4​M/δ)M)\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{\bar{r}L}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}+L_{\theta}\right)d^{\theta}\sqrt{\frac{\log(4M/\delta)}{M}}\right) |
